**Manhattan**[^cite_note-11] is the most densely populated and geographically smallest of the [five boroughs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boroughs_of_New_York_City) of [New York City](645042.md). Manhattan is coextensive with **New York County**, which is the [smallest county by area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_statistics_of_the_United_States#Smallest,_largest,_and_average_area_per_state_and_territory) in the U.S. state of [New York](8210131.md), and one of the [smallest in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_counties_and_county_equivalents). Located almost entirely on Manhattan Island near the southern tip of the state, Manhattan is centrally located in the [Northeast megalopolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_megalopolis) and represents the urban core of the [New York metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_metropolitan_area).[^cite_note-12] Manhattan serves as New York City's [economic](3848717.md) and [administrative](531637.md) center and has been described as the cultural, financial, [media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media_in_New_York_City), and [entertainment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Show_business) capital of the world.[^cite_note-13][^cite_note-14][^cite_note-15][^cite_note-ManhattanMediaEntertainmentCapital-16]

Before European colonization, present-day Manhattan was part of [Lenape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lenape) territory.[^cite_note-\:1-17] European settlement began with the establishment of a [trading post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trading_post) by [Dutch colonists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_colonization_of_the_Americas) in 1624 on Manhattan Island; the post was named "[New Amsterdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Amsterdam)" in 1626. The territory came under English control in 1664 and was renamed "New York" after King [Charles II of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_II_of_England) granted the lands to his brother, the [Duke of York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_II_of_England).[^cite_note-18] New York, based in present-day [Lower Manhattan](455646.md), served as the [capital of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_capitals_in_the_United_States#Capitals_of_the_US) from 1785 until 1790.[^cite_note-senate-19]

The [Statue of Liberty](28617.md) in [New York Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Harbor), part of Manhattan, greeted millions of arriving immigrants [in the late 19th century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_immigration_to_the_United_States#1850_to_1930) and symbolizes the United States and its ideals.[^cite_note-20] Manhattan became a borough during the [consolidation of New York City in 1898](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Greater_New_York), and houses [New York City Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_City_Hall), the seat of the [city's government](531637.md).[^cite_note-21] [Harlem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harlem) in [Upper Manhattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Manhattan) became the center of what is now known as the cultural [Harlem Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harlem_Renaissance) in the 1920s. The [Stonewall Inn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stonewall_Inn) in [Greenwich Village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenwich_Village), is considered the [birthplace in 1969](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stonewall_riots) of the modern [gay-rights movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_movements), cementing Manhattan's central role in [LGBTQ culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_culture_in_New_York_City).[^cite_note-NPS99000562-22][^cite_note-ObamaStonewall-23] Lower Manhattan was the site of the original [World Trade Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Center_\(1973%E2%80%932001\)), which was [destroyed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collapse_of_the_World_Trade_Center) during the [September 11 terrorist attacks](5058690.md) in 2001.

Situated on [one of the world's largest natural harbors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Harbor), the borough is bounded by the [Hudson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_River), [East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_River), and [Harlem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harlem_River) rivers and includes [several small adjacent islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_smaller_islands_in_New_York_City), including [Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roosevelt_Island), [U Thant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U_Thant_Island), and [Randalls and Wards Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Randalls_and_Wards_Islands). It also includes the small neighborhood of [Marble Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marble_Hill,_Manhattan), now on the [U.S. mainland](83759.md). Manhattan Island is divided into three informally bounded components, each cutting across the borough's long axis: [Lower](455646.md), [Midtown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midtown_Manhattan), and [Upper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Manhattan). Manhattan is one of the most densely populated locations in the world, with a [2020 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_census) population of 1,694,250 living in a land area of 22.66 square miles (58.69 km<sup>2</sup>),[^cite_note-ManhattanQuickFacts-6][^cite_note-24] or 72,918 residents per square mile (28,154 residents/km<sup>2</sup>).

Manhattan is known for its fast pace and continuous urban energy.[^cite_note-ManhattanFastPaced1-25][^cite_note-ManhattanFastPaced2-26][^cite_note-ManhattanFastPaced3-27][^cite_note-Manhattan24/7Economy-28] New York County is by a significant margin the most expensive [U.S. county](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_\(United_States\)) for the average home price per square foot,[^cite_note-ManhattanHighestMedianPricePerSquareFoot-29] and produces the [highest U.S. county GDP by both absolute and per capita measures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._counties_by_GDP). The borough houses [Billionaires' Row](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billionaires'_Row) and [Wall Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wall_Street), as well as the world's two [largest stock exchanges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_major_stock_exchanges) by total [market capitalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_capitalization), the [New York Stock Exchange](21560.md) and [Nasdaq](21559.md).[^cite_note-LargestExchanges-30] Many multinational [media conglomerates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_conglomerate) are based in Manhattan, as are numerous colleges and universities, including [Columbia University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_University), [New York University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_University), [Rockefeller University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockefeller_University), and the [City University of New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_University_of_New_York). The [headquarters of the United Nations](188955.md) is located in the [Turtle Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turtle_Bay,_Manhattan) neighborhood of Midtown Manhattan.

Manhattan hosts three of the world's top 10 most-visited tourist attractions: [Times Square](31389.md), [Central Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Park), and [Grand Central Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Central_Terminal).[^cite_note-Ann_Shields-31] [New York Penn Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Penn_Station) is the busiest transportation hub in the [Western Hemisphere](73162.md).[^cite_note-32] [Fifth Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifth_Avenue) is an ultra-luxury international shopping corridor that is undergoing additional infrastructure investment.[^cite_note-FifthAvenueUltraLuxuryInvestment-33] [Chinatown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinatown,_Manhattan) has the highest concentration of [Chinese people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_people_in_the_New_York_metropolitan_area) in the Western Hemisphere.[^cite_note-34]

The borough connects with many well-known [bridges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_bridges_and_tunnels_in_New_York_City) and [tunnels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_bridges_and_tunnels_in_New_York_City). Manhattan's numerous notable [skyscrapers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_New_York_City) include the [Empire State Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_State_Building), [Chrysler Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chrysler_Building), and [One World Trade Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_World_Trade_Center).[^cite_note-35] It is also home to the [National Basketball Association](22093.md)'s [New York Knicks](72855.md) and the [National Hockey League](21809.md)'s [New York Rangers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Rangers).

## History

Main article: [History of Manhattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Manhattan)

See also: [History of New York City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_New_York_City)

### Lenape settlement

Main article: [Lenape settlements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lenape_settlements)

Manhattan was historically part of the [Lenapehoking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lenapehoking) territory inhabited by the [Munsee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munsee), [Lenape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lenape),[^cite_note-36] and [Wappinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wappinger) [tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-nb-37] There were several Lenape settlements in the area including [Sapohanikan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sapohanikan), [Nechtanc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nechtanc), and [Konaande Kongh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konaande_Kongh), which were interconnected by a series of trails. The primary trail on the island, which would later become [Broadway](4626.md), ran from what is now [Inwood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inwood,_Manhattan) in the north to [Battery Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battery_Park) in the south.[^cite_note-38] There were various sites for fishing and planting established by the Lenape throughout Manhattan.[^cite_note-\:1-17]

#### Toponymy

Further information: [Etymology of Manhattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Etymology_of_Manhattan)

The [etymology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Etymology) of the name *Manhattan* is most likely – amongst other theories, and via [loaning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loanword) by [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_language) — from the [Lenape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lenape)'s local language [Munsee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munsee_language), *manaháhtaan* (where *manah-* means "gather", *-aht-* means "[bow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bow_\(archery\))", and *-aan* is used to form verb [stems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Word_stem)). The Lenape word has been translated as "the place where we get bows" or "place for gathering the (wood to make) bows". According to a Munsee tradition recorded by [Albert Seqaqkind Anthony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Seqaqkind_Anthony) in the 19th century, the island was named for a grove of [hickory trees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hickory_tree) that was considered ideal for bowmaking. An alternate theory claims a "[Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_languages) source akin to [Munsee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munsee) *munahan* ("island")."[^cite_note-39][^cite_note-40]

### Colonial era

Main articles: [New Netherland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Netherland), [New Amsterdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Amsterdam), and [Province of New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_New_York)



The [Castello Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castello_Plan), a 1660 map of New Amsterdam (the top right corner is roughly north) in [Lower Manhattan](455646.md)



[New Amsterdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Amsterdam), centered in what eventually became Lower Manhattan, in 1664, the year [England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_colonization_of_the_Americas) took control and renamed it New York

In April 1524, [Florentine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florence) explorer [Giovanni da Verrazzano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giovanni_da_Verrazzano), sailing in service of [Francis I of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_I_of_France), became the first documented European to visit the area that would become New York City.[^cite_note-41] Verrazzano entered the [tidal strait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_strait) now known as [The Narrows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Narrows) and named the land around [Upper New York Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_New_York_Harbor) *[New Angoulême](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Angoul%C3%AAme)*, in reference to the family name of [King Francis I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Francis_I); he sailed far enough into the harbor to sight the [Hudson River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_River), and he named the *Bay of Santa Margarita* – what is now [Upper New York Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_New_York_Bay) – after [Marguerite de Navarre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marguerite_de_Navarre), the elder sister of the king.[^cite_note-42][^cite_note-43]

Manhattan was first mapped during a 1609 voyage of [Henry Hudson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Hudson).[^cite_note-44] Hudson came across Manhattan Island and the native people living there, and continued up the river that would later bear his name, the [Hudson River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_River).[^cite_note-SciAm-45] Manhattan was first recorded in writing as *Manna-hata*, in the logbook of Robert Juet, an officer on the voyage.[^cite_note-46]

A permanent European presence in [New Netherland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Netherland) began in 1624, with the founding of a [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Republic) [fur trading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fur_trading) settlement on [Governors Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governors_Island).[^cite_note-47] In 1625, construction was started on the [citadel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citadel) of [Fort Amsterdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Amsterdam) on Manhattan Island, later called [New Amsterdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Amsterdam) (*Nieuw Amsterdam*), in what is now Lower Manhattan.[^cite_note-48][^cite_note-Tolerance-49][^cite_note-50] The establishment of Fort Amsterdam is recognized as the birth of New York City.[^cite_note-51] In 1647, [Peter Stuyvesant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Stuyvesant) was appointed as the last Dutch Director-General of the colony.[^cite_note-52] New Amsterdam was formally incorporated as a city on February 2, 1653.[^cite_note-53] In 1664, English forces conquered New Netherland and renamed it "New York" after the English [Duke of York and Albany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_II_of_England), the future King James II.[^cite_note-54] The [Treaty of Breda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Breda_\(1667\)), signed on July 31, 1667, ended the [Second Anglo-Dutch war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Anglo-Dutch_war). The Dutch ceded New Netherland (encompassing modern-day New York, New Jersey, Connecticut and Delaware) to England in exchange for the territory of [Suriname](26828.md) and control of the spice-producing [Island of Run](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Run_\(island\)) in the East Indies, known for its production of [nutmeg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nutmeg) and [mace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mace_\(spice\)).[^cite_note-55] In August 1673, the [Dutch reconquered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconquest_of_New_Netherland) the colony, renaming it "New Orange", but permanently relinquished it back to England the following year under the terms of the [Treaty of Westminster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Westminster_\(1674\)) that ended the [Third Anglo-Dutch War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Anglo-Dutch_War).[^cite_note-56][^cite_note-57]

### American Revolution

Further information: [American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolution)



[Statue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statue_of_George_Washington_\(Wall_Street\)) of [George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington) in front of [Federal Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Hall) on [Wall Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wall_Street), where in 1789 he was sworn in as the [first U.S. president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_George_Washington).

[^cite_note-58]

Manhattan was at the heart of the [New York Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Campaign), a series of major battles in the early stages of the [American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War). The [Continental Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Army) was forced to abandon Manhattan after the [Battle of Fort Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Fort_Washington) on November 16, 1776.[^cite_note-59] The city, greatly damaged by the [Great Fire of New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Fire_of_New_York_\(1776\)) during the campaign, became the British military and political center of operations in North America for the remainder of the war.[^cite_note-60] British occupation lasted until November 25, 1783, when [George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington) returned to Manhattan, a day celebrated as [Evacuation Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evacuation_Day_\(New_York\)), marking when the last British forces left the city.[^cite_note-61]

From January 11, 1785, until 1789, New York City was the fifth of five [capitals of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitals_of_the_United_States) under the [Articles of Confederation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Articles_of_Confederation), with the [Continental Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Congress) meeting at [New York City Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_City_Hall) (then at [Fraunces Tavern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fraunces_Tavern)).[^cite_note-62] New York was the first capital under the newly enacted [Constitution of the United States](31644.md), from March 4, 1789, to August 12, 1790, at [Federal Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Hall).[^cite_note-senate-19] Federal Hall was where the [United States Supreme Court](31737.md) met for the first time,[^cite_note-63] the [United States Bill of Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Bill_of_Rights) were drafted and ratified,[^cite_note-cwf-64] and where the [Northwest Ordinance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Ordinance) was adopted, establishing measures for [admission to the Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Admission_to_the_Union) of new states.[^cite_note-65]

### 19th century

New York grew as an economic center, first as a result of [Alexander Hamilton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Hamilton)'s policies and practices as the first [Secretary of the Treasury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_the_Treasury) to expand the city's role as a center of commerce and industry.[^cite_note-66] In 1810, New York City, then confined to Manhattan, had surpassed [Philadelphia](50585.md) as the most populous city in the United States.[^cite_note-67] The [Commissioners' Plan of 1811](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commissioners'_Plan_of_1811) laid out the island of Manhattan in its familiar [grid plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grid_plan).[^cite_note-MCNY1811-68] The city's role as an economic center grew with the opening of the [Erie Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erie_Canal) in 1825, cutting transportation costs by 90% compared to road transport and connecting the Atlantic port to the vast agricultural markets of the [Midwestern United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midwestern_United_States) and Canada.[^cite_note-69][^cite_note-lankevich-p67-70][^cite_note-71]

[Tammany Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tammany_Hall), a [Democratic Party](5043544.md) [political machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_machine), began to grow in influence with the support of many of the [immigrant Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_diaspora), culminating in the election of the first Tammany mayor, [Fernando Wood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Wood), in 1854.[^cite_note-72] Covering 840 acres (340 ha) in the center of the island, [Central Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Park), which opened its first portions to the public in 1858, became the first [landscaped public park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arboriculture) in an American city.[^cite_note-73][^cite_note-74][^cite_note-75][^cite_note-76]



The "Sanitary & Topographical Map of the City and Island of New York", commonly known as the [Viele Map](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viele_Map), developed by [Egbert Ludovicus Viele](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egbert_Ludovicus_Viele) in 1865

New York City played a complex role in the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War). The city had strong commercial ties to the [South](179553.md), but anger around [conscription](5735.md), resentment against Lincoln's war policies and paranoia about [free Blacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Blacks) taking the jobs of poor immigrants[^cite_note-77] culminated in the three-day-long [New York Draft riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Draft_riots) of July 1863, among the worst incidents of [civil disorder](329906.md) in American history.[^cite_note-78] The rate of immigration from Europe grew steeply after the Civil War, and Manhattan became the first stop for millions seeking a new life in the United States, a role acknowledged by the dedication of the [Statue of Liberty](28617.md) in 1886.[^cite_note-79][^cite_note-80] This immigration brought further social upheaval. In a city of tenements packed with poorly paid laborers from dozens of nations, the city became a hotbed of revolution (including anarchists and [communists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist) among others), [syndicalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syndicalism), [racketeering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racketeering), and [unionization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unionization).[^cite_note-81]

In 1883, the opening of the [Brooklyn Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brooklyn_Bridge) across the [East River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_River) established a road connection to [Brooklyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brooklyn) and the rest of [Long Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_Island).[^cite_note-82] In 1898, New York City consolidated with three neighboring counties to form "the [City of Greater New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Greater_New_York)", and Manhattan was established as one of the five [boroughs of New York City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boroughs_of_New_York_City).[^cite_note-83][^cite_note-84] [The Bronx](3338.md) remained part of New York County until 1914, when Bronx County was established.[^cite_note-85]

### 20th century

Further information: [Triangle Shirtwaist Factory fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triangle_Shirtwaist_Factory_fire) and [Stonewall riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stonewall_riots)



Manhattan's [Little Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Italy,_Manhattan) on the [Lower East Side](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_East_Side), c. 1900

A man working on a steel girder high above a city skyline.

[Construction workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Construction_worker) atop the [30 Rockefeller Plaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/30_Rockefeller_Plaza) building during its construction in 1932, in the photograph *[Lunch atop a Skyscraper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunch_atop_a_Skyscraper)*.

A two-story building with brick on the first floor, with two arched doorways, and gray stucco on the second floor, off of which hang numerous rainbow flags.

[Stonewall Inn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stonewall_Inn) in [Greenwich Village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenwich_Village), the site of the June 1969 [Stonewall riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stonewall_riots) and the cradle of the modern [LGBTQ+ rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ+_rights) movement

The construction of the [New York City Subway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_City_Subway), which opened in 1904, helped bind the consolidated city together,[^cite_note-86] as did the completion of the [Williamsburg Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Williamsburg_Bridge) (1903) and [Manhattan Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manhattan_Bridge) (1909) connecting to Brooklyn and the [Queensboro Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queensboro_Bridge) (1909) connecting to Queens.[^cite_note-87] In the 1920s, Manhattan experienced large arrivals of African-Americans as part of the [Great Migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Migration_\(African_American\)) from the southern United States, and the [Harlem Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harlem_Renaissance),[^cite_note-88] part of a larger boom time in the [Prohibition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prohibition_in_the_United_States) era that included new skyscrapers competing for the skyline, with the [Woolworth Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woolworth_Building) (1913), [40 Wall Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/40_Wall_Street) (1930), the [Chrysler Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chrysler_Building) (1930), and the [Empire State Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_State_Building) (1931) leapfrogging each other to take their place as the [world's tallest building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_world's_tallest_buildings).[^cite_note-89]

Manhattan's majority [white](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_American) ethnic group declined from 98.7% in 1900 to 58.3% by 1990.[^cite_note-Census1790to1990-90] On March 25, 1911, the [Triangle Shirtwaist Factory fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triangle_Shirtwaist_Factory_fire) in [Greenwich Village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenwich_Village) killed 146 [garment workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garment_worker),[^cite_note-91] leading to overhauls of the city's fire department, [building codes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Building_code), and workplace safety regulations.[^cite_note-92] In 1912, about 20,000 workers, a quarter of them women, marched upon [Washington Square Park](745249.md) to commemorate the fire. Many of the women wore fitted tucked-front blouses like those manufactured by the company, a clothing style that became the working woman's uniform and a symbol of [women's liberation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_liberation), reflecting the alliance of the labor and [suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suffrage) movements.[^cite_note-93]

Despite the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression), some of the world's tallest skyscrapers were completed in Manhattan during the 1930s, including numerous [Art Deco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Deco) masterpieces that are still part of the city's skyline, most notably the Empire State Building, the Chrysler Building, and [30 Rockefeller Plaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/30_Rockefeller_Plaza).[^cite_note-94] During [World War II](32927.md), the borough gave its name to the [Manhattan Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manhattan_Project), the top-secret U.S.-led research program that developed the first [nuclear weapons](21785.md). The project was named after the Manhattan Engineer District of the [U.S. Army Corps of Engineers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Army_Corps_of_Engineers), which was initially headquartered in Manhattan.[^cite_note-95][^cite_note-96] A postwar economic boom led to the development of huge housing developments targeted at returning veterans, the largest being [Stuyvesant Town–Peter Cooper Village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stuyvesant_Town%E2%80%93Peter_Cooper_Village), which opened in 1947.[^cite_note-97][^cite_note-98] The United Nations relocated to a new [headquarters](188955.md) that was completed in 1952 along the East River.[^cite_note-99][^cite_note-100][^cite_note-101]

The [Stonewall riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stonewall_riots) were a series of spontaneous, violent protests by members of the [gay community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gay_community) against a [police raid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_raid) that took place in the early morning hours of June 28, 1969, at the [Stonewall Inn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stonewall_Inn) in the [Greenwich Village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenwich_Village) neighborhood of Lower Manhattan. They are widely considered to constitute the single most important event leading to the [gay liberation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gay_liberation) movement[^cite_note-GayGreenwichVillage1-102][^cite_note-KentuckyStonewall-103] and the modern fight for [LGBT rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights).[^cite_note-NPSStonewall-104][^cite_note-ObamaStonewall-23][^cite_note-GayborhoodGreenwichVillage-105][^cite_note-NPS99000562-22][^cite_note-106] In the 1970s, job losses due to [industrial restructuring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deindustrialization) caused New York City, including Manhattan, to suffer from economic problems and rising crime rates.[^cite_note-107] While a resurgence in the [financial industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_industry) greatly improved the city's economic health in the 1980s, New York's crime rate continued to increase through the decade and into the beginning of the 1990s.[^cite_note-108] The 1980s saw a rebirth of [Wall Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wall_Street), and Manhattan reclaimed its role as the world's [financial center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_center), with Wall Street employment doubling from 1977 to 1987.[^cite_note-109] The 1980s also saw Manhattan at the heart of the [AIDS crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIDS_crisis), with Greenwich Village at its epicenter.[^cite_note-110]

In the 1970s, [Times Square](31389.md) and [42nd Street](61068.md) – with its sex shops, [peep shows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peep_show), and adult theaters, along with its [sex trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prostitution), street crime, and public drug use – became emblematic of the city's decline, with a 1981 article in *[Rolling Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rolling_Stone)* magazine calling the stretch of West 42nd Street between [7th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/7th_Avenue_\(Manhattan\)) and [8th Avenues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/8th_Avenue_\(Manhattan\)) the "sleaziest block in America".[^cite_note-111] By the late 1990s, led by efforts by the city and the [Walt Disney Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Disney_Company), the area had been revived as a center of tourism to the point where it was described by *[The New York Times](30680.md)* as "arguably the most sought-after 13 acres of commercial property in the world."[^cite_note-112]

By the 1990s, crime rates began to drop dramatically[^cite_note-113][^cite_note-114] and the city once again became the destination of immigrants from around the world, joining with low interest rates and Wall Street [bonus payments](25539917.md) to fuel the growth of the real estate market.[^cite_note-115] Important new sectors, such as [Silicon Alley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicon_Alley), emerged in the [Flatiron District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flatiron_District), cementing technology as a key component of Manhattan's economy.[^cite_note-116] The [1993 World Trade Center bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_World_Trade_Center_bombing), described by the [FBI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FBI) as "something of a deadly dress rehearsal for 9/11", was a terrorist attack in which six people were killed when a van bomb filled with explosives was detonated in a parking lot below the [North Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tenants_in_1_World_Trade_Center_\(1971%E2%80%932001\)) of the [World Trade Center complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Center_\(1973%E2%80%932001\)).[^cite_note-117]

### 21st century

See also: [September 11 attacks](5058690.md)



[United Airlines Flight 175](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Airlines_Flight_175) hits the [South Tower](19372810.md) on September 11, 2001.

On September 11, 2001, the [Twin Towers of the original World Trade Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Center_\(1973%E2%80%932001\)) were struck by hijacked aircraft and collapsed in the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md) launched by [al-Qaeda](1921.md) terrorists. The collapse caused extensive damage to surrounding buildings and skyscrapers in Lower Manhattan, and resulted in the [deaths of 2,606](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casualties_of_the_September_11_attacks) of the 17,400 who had been in the buildings when the planes hit, in addition to those on the planes.[^cite_note-118] Since 2001, most of [Lower Manhattan](455646.md) has been restored, although [there has been controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Center_rebuilding_controversy) surrounding the rebuilding. In 2014, the new [One World Trade Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_World_Trade_Center), at 1,776 feet (541 m) measured to the top of its spire, became the tallest building in the Western Hemisphere[^cite_note-119] and is the [world's seventh-tallest building (as of 2023)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings).[^cite_note-120]

The [Occupy Wall Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupy_Wall_Street) protests in [Zuccotti Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zuccotti_Park) in the [Financial District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_District_\(Manhattan\)) of Lower Manhattan began on September 17, 2011, receiving global attention and spawning the [Occupy movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupy_movement) against [social](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_inequality) and [economic inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_inequality) worldwide.[^cite_note-121][^cite_note-122]

On October 29 and 30, 2012, [Hurricane Sandy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Sandy) caused [extensive destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effects_of_Hurricane_Sandy_in_New_York) in the borough, ravaging portions of Lower Manhattan with record-high [storm surge](449744.md) from New York Harbor,[^cite_note-NYC_after_Sandy-123] severe flooding, and high winds, causing [power outages](296636.md) for hundreds of thousands of city residents[^cite_note-124] and leading to gasoline shortages[^cite_note-125] and disruption of [mass transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_transit) systems.[^cite_note-126][^cite_note-127][^cite_note-128][^cite_note-129] The storm and its profound impacts have prompted discussion of constructing [seawalls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seawall) and other [coastal barriers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coastal_management) around the [shorelines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shoreline) of the borough and the metropolitan area to minimize the risk of destructive consequences from another such event in the future.[^cite_note-130]

## Geography

See also: [Geography of New York City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_New_York_City)



A satellite image of Manhattan, bounded by the [Hudson River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_River) to the west, the [Harlem River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harlem_River) to the north, the [East River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_River) to the east, and [New York Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Harbor) to the south, with rectangular [Central Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Park) prominently visible. [Roosevelt Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roosevelt_Island), in the East River, belongs to Manhattan.

According to 2020 data from the [United States Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census_Bureau), New York County had a total area of 33.859 sq mi (87.69 km<sup>2</sup>), of which 22.656 sq mi (58.68 km<sup>2</sup>) is land and 11.203 sq mi (29.02 km<sup>2</sup>) (33.1%) is water.[^cite_note-CensusArea-3] The northern segment of Upper Manhattan represents a geographic [panhandle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panhandle). Manhattan Island is 22.7 sq mi (59 km<sup>2</sup>) in area, 13.4 mi (21.6 km) long and 2.3 mi (3.7 km) wide, at its widest point, near [14th Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/14th_Street_\(Manhattan\)).[^cite_note-Passikoff-4][^cite_note-131]

The borough consists primarily of Manhattan Island, along with the [Marble Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marble_Hill,_Manhattan) neighborhood and several small islands, including [Randalls Island and Wards Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Randalls_and_Wards_Islands) and [Roosevelt Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roosevelt_Island) in the East River; and [Governors Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governors_Island) and [Liberty Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberty_Island) to the south in [New York Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Harbor).[^cite_note-Islands-132]

### Manhattan Island

The Island is about 13.4 mi (21.6 km) from north to south, and at its widest, 2.3 mi (3.7 km).[^cite_note-Passikoff-4] Manhattan Island is loosely divided into Downtown ([Lower Manhattan](455646.md)), Midtown ([Midtown Manhattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midtown_Manhattan)), and Uptown ([Upper Manhattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Manhattan)), with [Fifth Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifth_Avenue) dividing Manhattan lengthwise into its [East Side](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Side_\(Manhattan\)) and [West Side](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Side_\(Manhattan\)).[^cite_note-133] Manhattan Island is bounded by the [Hudson River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_River) to the west and the [East River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_River) to the east. To the north, the [Harlem River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harlem_River) divides Manhattan Island from [the Bronx](3338.md) and the mainland United States.[^cite_note-134]

Early in the 19th century, [land reclamation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_reclamation) was used to expand Lower Manhattan from the natural Hudson shoreline at [Greenwich Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenwich_Street) to [West Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Street).[^cite_note-135] When [building the World Trade Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Building_the_World_Trade_Center) in 1968, 1.2 million cubic yards (32,000,000 cu ft; 920,000 m<sup>3</sup>) of material excavated from the site[^cite_note-136] was used to expand the Manhattan [shoreline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shoreline) across West Street, creating [Battery Park City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battery_Park_City).[^cite_note-137] Constructed on piers at a cost of \$260 million, [Little Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Island_at_Pier_55) opened on the Hudson River in May 2021, connected to the western termini of [13th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/13th_Street_\(Manhattan\)) and [14th Streets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/14th_Street_\(Manhattan\)) by [footbridges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Footbridge).[^cite_note-138]

### Marble Hill

Main article: [Marble Hill, Manhattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marble_Hill,_Manhattan)

Marble Hill was part of the northern tip of Manhattan Island, but the [Harlem River Ship Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harlem_River_Ship_Canal), dug in 1895 to better connect the [Harlem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harlem_River) and [Hudson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_River) rivers, separated it from the remainder of Manhattan.[^cite_note-canal-139] Before World War I, the section of the original Harlem River channel separating Marble Hill from the Bronx was filled in, and Marble Hill became part of the mainland.[^cite_note-140] After a May 1984 court ruling that Marble Hill was simultaneously part of the Borough of Manhattan (not the Borough of the Bronx) and part of Bronx County (not New York County),[^cite_note-141] the matter was definitively settled later that year when the [New York Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Legislature) overwhelmingly passed legislation declaring the neighborhood part of both New York County and the Borough of Manhattan.[^cite_note-142][^cite_note-143]

### Smaller islands

See also: [List of smaller islands in New York City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_smaller_islands_in_New_York_City)

A tall green statue on an island in a harbor.

[Liberty Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberty_Island), an [exclave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclave) of Manhattan, New York City, and the [state of New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_New_York), that is surrounded by [New Jersey](21648.md) waters

Within [New York Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Harbor), there are three smaller islands:

- [Ellis Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellis_Island), shared with [New Jersey](21648.md)

  [^cite_note-144]

- [Governors Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governors_Island)

  [^cite_note-145]

- [Liberty Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberty_Island) (administered by the [National Park Service](161535.md))

  [^cite_note-146]

Other smaller islands, in the [East River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_River), include (from north to south):

- [Randalls and Wards Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Randalls_and_Wards_Islands), joined by landfill

- [Mill Rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mill_Rock)

- [Roosevelt Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roosevelt_Island), which has a population of 14,000, extends for 2 miles (3.2 km), and was renamed in 1973 from Welfare Island to honor President [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt).

  [^cite_note-147]

- [U Thant Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U_Thant_Island) (legally Belmont Island)

### Geology

Main article: [Geology of Manhattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geology_of_Manhattan)

The [bedrock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bedrock) underlying much of Manhattan consists of three rock formations: *Inwood [marble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marble)*, *Fordham [gneiss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gneiss)*, and *Manhattan [schist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schist)*, and is well suited for the foundations of Manhattan's [skyscrapers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skyscrapers).[^cite_note-148] It is part of the [Manhattan Prong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manhattan_Prong) physiographic region.

### Adjacent counties

### Climate

See also: [Climate of New York City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_New_York_City)



[Central Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Park) in autumn

Under the [Köppen climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification), New York City features both a [humid subtropical climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_subtropical_climate) (*Cfa*) and a [humid continental climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_continental_climate) (*Dfa*).[^cite_note-149] It is the northernmost major city on the North American continent with a humid subtropical climate. The city averages 234 days with at least some sunshine annually.[^cite_note-noaasun-150]

Winters are cold and damp, and prevailing wind patterns that blow offshore temper the moderating effects of the Atlantic Ocean. The Atlantic and the partial shielding from colder air by the [Appalachians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appalachians) keep the city warmer in the winter than inland North American cities at similar or lesser latitudes. The daily mean temperature in January, the area's coldest month, is 32.6 °F (0.3 °C).[^cite_note-New_York_City_Weatherbox_NOAA_txt-151] Temperatures usually drop to 10 °F (−12 °C) several times per winter,[^cite_note-New_York_City_Weatherbox_NOAA_txt-151][^cite_note-NYC_climate-152] and reach 60 °F (16 °C) several days in the coldest winter month.[^cite_note-New_York_City_Weatherbox_NOAA_txt-151] Spring and autumn are unpredictable and can range from chilly to warm, although they are usually mild with low humidity. Summers are typically warm to hot and humid, with a daily mean temperature of 76.5 °F (24.7 °C) in July.[^cite_note-New_York_City_Weatherbox_NOAA_txt-151]

Nighttime conditions are often exacerbated by the [urban heat island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_heat_island) phenomenon, which causes heat absorbed during the day to be radiated back at night, raising temperatures by as much as 7 °F (4 °C) when winds are slow.[^cite_note-153] Daytime temperatures exceed 90 °F (32 °C) on average of 17 days each summer[^cite_note-New_York_City_Weatherbox_NOAA-154] and in some years exceed 100 °F (38 °C). Extreme temperatures have ranged from −15 °F (−26 °C), recorded on February 9, 1934, up to 106 °F (41 °C) on July 9, 1936.[^cite_note-New_York_City_Weatherbox_NOAA-154] Manhattan lies in USDA [plant hardiness zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plant_hardiness_zone) 7b (5 to 10 °F/-15 to -12.2 °C).[^cite_note-155]

Manhattan receives 49.9 inches (1,270 mm) of [precipitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precipitation) annually, which is relatively evenly spread throughout the year. Average winter snowfall between 1981 and 2010 has been 25.8 inches (66 cm). This varies considerably from year to year.[^cite_note-New_York_City_Weatherbox_NOAA-154]

### Neighborhoods

Main articles: [Neighborhoods in New York City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neighborhoods_in_New_York_City) and [List of Manhattan neighborhoods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Manhattan_neighborhoods)



The [Empire State Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_State_Building) in the foreground, looking south from the top of [Rockefeller Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockefeller_Center) with [One World Trade Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_World_Trade_Center) in the background

Manhattan's many neighborhoods are not named according to any particular convention, nor do they have official boundaries. Some are geographical (the [Upper East Side](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_East_Side)), or ethnically descriptive ([Little Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Italy,_Manhattan)). Others are [acronyms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acronyms), such as [TriBeCa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribeca) (for "TRIangle BElow CAnal Street") or [SoHo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SoHo) ("SOuth of HOuston"), [NoLIta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NoLIta) ("NOrth of Little ITAly"), and [NoMad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NoMad) ("NOrth of MADison Square Park").[^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161][^cite_note-162][^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164] [Harlem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harlem) is a name from the Dutch colonial era after [Haarlem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haarlem), a city in the Netherlands.[^cite_note-165] Some have simple [folkloric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folkloric) names, such as [Hell's Kitchen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hell's_Kitchen), alongside their more official but lesser used title (in this case, Clinton).[^cite_note-166]

Some neighborhoods, such as [SoHo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SoHo), which is mixed use, are known for [upscale shopping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upscale_shopping) as well as residential use.[^cite_note-167] Others, such as [Greenwich Village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenwich_Village), the [Lower East Side](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_East_Side), [Alphabet City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alphabet_City) and the [East Village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Village,_Manhattan), have long been associated with the [Bohemian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bohemianism) subculture.[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-169][^cite_note-170] [Chelsea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chelsea,_Manhattan) is one of several Manhattan neighborhoods with large [gay populations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_culture_in_New_York_City) and has become a center of both the international [art industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visual_art) and New York's nightlife.[^cite_note-171] [Chinatown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinatown,_Manhattan) has the highest concentration of people of [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Americans_in_New_York_City) descent outside of Asia.[^cite_note-172][^cite_note-173]

[Koreatown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koreatown,_Manhattan) is roughly centered on 32nd Street between Fifth and Sixth Avenues.[^cite_note-KoreatownExpanding-174] [Rose Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rose_Hill,_Manhattan) features a growing number of Indian restaurants and spice shops along a stretch of [Lexington Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lexington_Avenue) between 25th and 30th Streets which has become known as *[Curry Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curry_Hill)*.[^cite_note-175] [Washington Heights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Heights,_Manhattan) in [Uptown Manhattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uptown_Manhattan) is home to the largest [Dominican immigrant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominican_Americans#New_York_City) community in the United States.[^cite_note-176] [Harlem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harlem), also in Upper Manhattan, is the historical epicenter of African American culture.[^cite_note-177] Since 2010, a *[Little Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Australia)* has emerged and is growing in Nolita, Lower Manhattan.[^cite_note-LittleAustraliaNYC-178]

Manhattan has two central business districts, the [Financial District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_District,_Manhattan) at the southern tip of the island, and [Midtown Manhattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midtown_Manhattan). The term *uptown* also refers to the northern part of Manhattan above [72nd Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/72nd_Street_\(Manhattan\)) and *downtown* to the southern portion below [14th Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/14th_Street_\(Manhattan\)),[^cite_note-NYCBasics-179] with *Midtown* covering the area in between, though definitions can be fluid. [Fifth Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifth_Avenue) roughly bisects Manhattan Island and acts as the demarcation line for east/west designations.[^cite_note-NYCBasics-179][^cite_note-180] South of [Waverly Place](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waverly_Place), Fifth Avenue terminates and Broadway becomes the east/west demarcation line. In Manhattan, *uptown* means north and *downtown* means south.[^cite_note-ManhattanUptownDowntown-181] This usage differs from that of most American cities, where *downtown* refers to the central business district.

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of Manhattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Manhattan)

Looking at crowds down Broadway

[Broadway](4626.md) in [Midtown Manhattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midtown_Manhattan). As of the [2020 U.S. census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_U.S._census), Manhattan was the most densely populated municipality in the United States.

As of the [2020 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_census), Manhattan's population had increased by 6.8% over the decade to 1,694,251, representing 19.2% of New York City's population of 8,804,190 and 8.4% of New York State's population of 20,201,249.[^cite_note-ManhattanQuickFacts-6] The population density of New York County was 70,450.8 inhabitants per square mile (27,201.2/km<sup>2</sup>) in 2022, the highest population density of any county in the United States and [higher than the density of any individual U.S. city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_cities_by_population_density#New_York_City_boroughs).[^cite_note-182][^cite_note-183] At the [2010 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_United_States_census), there were 1,585,873 people living in Manhattan, an increase of 3.2% from the 1,537,195 counted in the [2000 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_United_States_census).[^cite_note-184]

### Religion

In 2010, the largest organized religious group in Manhattan was the [Archdiocese of New York](245234.md), with 323,325 Catholics worshiping at 109 parishes, followed by 64,000 [Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Judaism) Jews with 77 congregations, an estimated 42,545 [Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_the_United_States) with 21 congregations, 42,502 [non-denominational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nondenominational_Christianity) adherents with 54 congregations, 26,178 [TEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopal_Church_\(United_States\)) Episcopalians with 46 congregations, 25,048 [ABC-USA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Baptist_Churches_USA) Baptists with 41 congregations, 24,536 [Reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Judaism) Jews with 10 congregations, 23,982 [Mahayana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahayana) Buddhists with 35 congregations, 10,503 [PC-USA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PC-USA) Presbyterians with 30 congregations, and 10,268 [RCA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformed_Church_in_America) Presbyterians with 10 congregations. Altogether, 44.0% of the population was claimed as members by religious congregations, although members of historically African-American denominations were underrepresented due to incomplete information.[^cite_note-189] In 2014, Manhattan had 703 religious organizations, the seventeenth most out of all US counties.[^cite_note-190] There is a large [Buddhist temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_temple) in Manhattan located at the foot of the [Manhattan Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manhattan_Bridge) in [Chinatown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinatown,_Manhattan).[^cite_note-191]

### Languages

As of 2015, 60.0% (927,650) of Manhattan residents, aged five and older, spoke only English at home, while 22.63% (350,112) spoke Spanish, 5.37% (83,013) Chinese, 2.21% (34,246) French, 0.85% (13,138) Korean, 0.72% (11,135) Russian, and 0.70% (10,766) Japanese. In total, 40.0% of Manhattan's population, aged five and older, spoke a language other than English at home.[^cite_note-192]

## Landmarks and architecture

Main article: [Architecture of New York City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_New_York_City)

See also: [List of skyscrapers in New York City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_skyscrapers_in_New_York_City)

Points of interest on Manhattan Island include the [American Museum of Natural History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Museum_of_Natural_History); the [Battery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Battery_\(Manhattan\)); [Broadway](4626.md) and the [Theater District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theater_District,_Manhattan); [Bryant Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bryant_Park); [Central Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Park), [Chinatown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinatown,_Manhattan); the [Chrysler Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chrysler_Building); [The Cloisters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Cloisters); [Columbia University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_University); [Curry Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curry_Hill); the [Empire State Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_State_Building); [Flatiron Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flatiron_Building); the [Financial District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_District,_Manhattan) (including the [New York Stock Exchange Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Stock_Exchange_Building); [Wall Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wall_Street); and the [South Street Seaport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Street_Seaport)); [Grand Central Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Central_Terminal); [Greenwich Village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenwich_Village) (including [New York University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_University); [Washington Square Arch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Square_Arch); and [Stonewall Inn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stonewall_Inn)); [Harlem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harlem) and [Spanish Harlem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Harlem); the [High Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Line); [Koreatown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koreatown,_Manhattan); [Lincoln Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Center); [Little Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Australia,_Manhattan); [Little Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Italy,_Manhattan); [Madison Square Garden](75129.md); [Museum Mile on Fifth Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifth_Avenue#Museum_Mile) (including the [Metropolitan Museum of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Museum_of_Art)); [New York Penn Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Penn_Station), [Port Authority Bus Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Authority_Bus_Terminal); [Rockefeller Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockefeller_Center) (including [Radio City Music Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_City_Music_Hall)); [Times Square](31389.md); and the [World Trade Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Center_\(2001%E2%80%93present\)) (including the [National September 11 Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_September_11_Museum) and [One World Trade Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_World_Trade_Center)).

There are also numerous iconic [bridges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bridges_and_tunnels_in_New_York_City) across rivers that connect to Manhattan Island, as well as an emerging number of [supertall skyscrapers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_New_York_City). The [Statue of Liberty](28617.md) rests on [Liberty Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberty_Island), an [exclave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclave) of Manhattan, and part of [Ellis Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellis_Island) is also an exclave of Manhattan. The borough has many [energy-efficient](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Efficient_energy_use) office buildings, such as the [Hearst Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hearst_Tower_\(New_York_City\)), the rebuilt [7 World Trade Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/7_World_Trade_Center),[^cite_note-greenbuilding-193] and the [Bank of America Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_America_Tower_\(Manhattan\))—the first skyscraper designed to attain a Platinum [LEED](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LEED) Certification.[^cite_note-194][^cite_note-195]



Many tall buildings have [setbacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Setback_\(architecture\)) on their facade due to the [1916 Zoning Resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1916_Zoning_Resolution), exemplified at [Park Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Park_Avenue) and [57th Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/57th_Street_\(Manhattan\)) in [Midtown Manhattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midtown_Manhattan).

The skyscraper, which has shaped Manhattan's distinctive [skyline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skyline), has been closely associated with New York City's identity since the end of the 19th century.[^cite_note-196] Structures such as the [Equitable Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equitable_Building_\(Manhattan\)) of 1915, which rises vertically forty stories from the sidewalk, prompted the passage of the [1916 Zoning Resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1916_Zoning_Resolution), requiring new buildings to contain [setbacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Setback_\(architecture\)) withdrawing progressively at a defined angle from the street as they rose, to preserve a view of the sky at street level.[^cite_note-nyt20160726-197] Manhattan's skyline includes several buildings that are symbolic of New York, in particular the [Chrysler Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chrysler_Building)[^cite_note-Willis_Friedman-198]<sup>: 14 </sup> and the [Empire State Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_State_Building), which sees about 4 million visitors a year.[^cite_note-nyt20111225-199]

In 1961, the struggling [Pennsylvania Railroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Railroad) unveiled plans to tear down the old [Penn Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Station_\(1910%E2%80%931963\)) and replace it with a new [Madison Square Garden](75129.md) and [office building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Penn_Plaza) complex.[^cite_note-200] Organized protests were aimed at preserving the [McKim, Mead & White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McKim,_Mead_&_White)-designed structure completed in 1910, widely considered a masterpiece of the [Beaux-Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beaux-Arts_architecture) style and one of the architectural jewels of New York City.[^cite_note-201] Despite these efforts, demolition of the structure began in October 1963.[^cite_note-202] The loss of Penn Station led directly to the enactment in 1965 of a local law establishing the [New York City Landmarks Preservation Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_City_Landmarks_Preservation_Commission), which is responsible for preserving the "city's historic, aesthetic, and [cultural heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_heritage)".[^cite_note-203] The [historic preservation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historic_preservation) movement triggered by Penn Station's demise has been credited with the retention of some one million structures nationwide, including over 1,000 in New York City.[^cite_note-204] In 2017, a multibillion-dollar rebuilding plan was unveiled to restore the historic grandeur of Penn Station, in the process of upgrading the landmark's status as a critical [transportation hub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation_hub).[^cite_note-205]

The 700,000 ft<sup>2</sup> (65,000 m<sup>2</sup>) [Moynihan Train Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moynihan_Train_Hall), developed as a \$1.6 billion renovation and expansion of Penn Station into the [James A. Farley Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_A._Farley_Building), the city's former main post office building, was opened in January 2021.[^cite_note-206]

### Parkland



[Central Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Park)

Parkland covers a total of 2,659 acres (10.76 km<sup>2</sup>), accounting for 18.2% of the borough's land area; the 840-acre (3.4 km<sup>2</sup>) [Central Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Park) is the borough's largest park, comprising 31.6% of Manhattan's parkland.[^cite_note-ParkList-207] Designed by [Frederick Law Olmsted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_Law_Olmsted) and [Calvert Vaux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calvert_Vaux), the park is anchored by the 12-acre (4.9 ha) [Great Lawn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lawn)[^cite_note-208] and offers extensive [walking tracks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walking_track), two [ice-skating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice-skating) rinks, a wildlife sanctuary, and several lawns and sporting areas, as well as 21 playgrounds,[^cite_note-209] and a 6-mile (9.7 km) road from which automobile traffic has been banned since 2018.[^cite_note-210] While much of the park looks natural, it is almost entirely landscaped; the [construction of Central Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Construction_of_Central_Park) in the 1850s was one of the era's most massive public works projects, with some 20,000 workers moving 5 million cubic yards (3.8 million cubic meters) of material to shape the [topography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topography) and create the English-style pastoral landscape that Olmsted and Vaux sought.[^cite_note-211]

The remaining 70% of Manhattan's parkland includes 204 playgrounds, 251 Greenstreets, 371 [basketball courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basketball_court), and many other amenities.[^cite_note-MBPOEnvironment-212] The next-largest park in Manhattan, the [Hudson River Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_River_Park), stretches 4.5 miles (7.2 km) along the Hudson River and comprises 550 acres (220 ha).[^cite_note-RIoG_ParkStats-213] Other major parks include:[^cite_note-ParkList-207]

- [Bowling Green](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bowling_Green_\(New_York_City\))
